| 1081 | `teacherScript` | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, Thank you for listening. Are there any questions? Dr. Vale and Lumi wave goodbye at the door, and Ms. Emma leads the four of us along a path of glowing pages. Soon, we step into a huge maze. The walls are tall bookshelves and hedges with paper leaves. Warm lanterns glimmer everywhere, and signposts show story titles. Then a small gray mouse in a red cape runs up to us. He is carrying a feather pen and a heavy old book. He bows and says, Welcome to the Labyrinth of Tales! I'm Nib, your guide. Every path in this maze tells a story. Say it with me. Every path in this maze tells a story. Great! Nib opens his book. It's the Storybook of the Labyrinth, and its last six pages are completely blank. Let's read the note on the first page. "Welcome, travelers! This is the Labyrinth of Tales. Its walls are made of bookshelves and hedges, and every path leads to a room where a story lives. In the middle of the maze is the Central Hall, where the Storytelling Festival will be held. This Storybook is very old, but it isn't finished yet. Its last six pages are still blank. They are waiting for friends who can learn the six secrets of stories. Each time you learn a secret, one blank page will fill with shining words. When all six pages are full, the Storybook will be complete. Please stay together, follow the lanterns, and listen carefully. And then what happened? That part is up to you!" Where will the Storytelling Festival be held? In the Central Hall. How many pages are still blank? Six. Far away, an old voice is telling a story. Nib says, That's Granny Willa. She sits by the fire in the Hearth Room. You'll meet her one day. Then he asks his favorite question, And then what happened? That's his catchphrase! Now, phonics. Strong verbs show how something happens. Whisper means to speak very quietly, using your breath. Whisper begins with w-h, but it sounds like w. Glimmer means to shine with a weak light that comes and goes. Tremble means to shake a little because you are scared, cold or excited. Tremble ends in l-e, and it sounds like bull. | 1081 | `phonics.tipJa` | 物語では「どう やって」が わかる 動詞を つかうよ。whisper（小さな 声で ささやく。wh は /w/ の 音）、glimmer（ちらちら かすかに ひかる。mm と 2 つ）、tremble（こわくて・さむくて ぶるぶる ふるえる。-le は「ブル」の ような 音）。 | 物語では「どう やって」が わかる 動詞を つかうよ。whisper（小さな 声で ささやく。wh は /w/ の 音）、glimmer（ちらちら かすかに ひかる。mm と 2 つ）、tremble（こわくて・さむくて ぶるぶる ふるえる。-ble は「ブル」の ような 音）。 | B+C | the 'ブル' sound is the b-l-e ending, not -le alone |

| 1082 | `teacherScript` | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, Every path in this maze tells a story. Today, Ms. Emma and Nib lead us down a path with a signpost that says The Clever Rabbit. At the end, there is a cozy room with a round green door. Inside, the walls are painted with tall grass and a big orange sunset. Nib jumps onto a stool, opens the Storybook and says, Many countries have stories about clever animals. Here is one from the Labyrinth. Listen carefully! It's about a tiny rabbit and a big, hungry tiger. The tiger is much stronger, but the rabbit has something better: a plan. The rabbit tricked the tiger with a smart idea. Say it with me. The rabbit tricked the tiger with a smart idea. Great! Let's read the story page. "Once upon a time, a little rabbit was hopping home with a basket of carrots. Suddenly, a big tiger stepped onto the path. He was hungry, and he was very grumpy. Give me those carrots, he growled. The rabbit's legs began to tremble, but she thought fast. Oh, these are magic carrots, she said. They only taste sweet if you close your eyes and count to one thousand. The tiger wanted the sweetest carrots in the world, so he closed his eyes. One, two, three... While the tiger was counting, the rabbit hopped quietly home. When the tiger reached one thousand, he opened his eyes. The rabbit was gone! At first, he was angry. Then he started to laugh. What a clever trick! he said. The next day, he knocked on the rabbit's door and asked nicely. So she shared her carrots with him." Why did the tiger close his eyes? Because he wanted the carrots to taste sweet. What did the tiger learn? He learned to ask nicely. Haru gasps and says, Count to one thousand? That would take forever! Birimo tries it and gives up at twelve. Nib smiles and asks, And then what happened? Now, phonics. Gasp means to breathe in quickly because you are surprised. Haru gasped when he heard the trick. Stumble means to almost fall while you are walking. Stumble ends in l-e, just like tremble. Glance means to look very quickly. When the tiger opened his eyes, he glanced around, but the rabbit was gone! Next time, we'll find out why the moon seems to change its shape. | Hello, everyone! Birimo tries it and gives up at twelve. Nib smiles and asks, And then what happened? Now, phonics. Gasp means to breathe in quickly because you are surprised. Haru gasped when he heard the trick. Stumble means to almost fall while you are walking. Stumble ends in l-e, just like tremble. Glance means to look very quickly. When the tiger opened his eyes, he glanced around, but the rabbit was gone! | 1082 | `phonics.tipJa` | gasp（おどろいて はっと 息を のむ）、stumble（つまずいて よろける。tremble と おなじ -le で「ブル」）、glance（ちらっと 見る。look より ずっと はやい）。ふつうの ことばより ようすが よく わかるね。 | gasp（おどろいて はっと 息を のむ）、stumble（つまずいて よろける。tremble と おなじ -ble で「ブル」）、glance（ちらっと 見る。look より ずっと はやい）。ふつうの ことばより ようすが よく わかるね。 | B+C | the 'ブル' sound is the b-l-e ending, not -le alone (same fix as L1081) |
| 1083 | `teacherScript` | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, The rabbit tricked the tiger with a smart idea. Today, Nib takes us to a dark blue room with a silver ceiling. Tiny stars sparkle above us, and a big, round moon lamp hangs in the middle. This is the Moon Room, Nib whispers. Long ago, people looked up at the night sky and wondered. Why is the moon round one week and thin the next? So they made up stories. Long ago, people told stories to explain the moon. Say it with me. Long ago, people told stories to explain the moon. Great! Let's read the old tale on the wall. "Why the Moon Changes. Long ago, people said that the moon was the kindest light in the sky. Every night, it watched over the sleepy animals below. Some of them were afraid of the dark. The little rabbits would shiver in their holes, and the baby deer couldn't sleep. So the moon broke off a piece of its own light and gave it to them. The next night, it gave away another piece, and then another. Little by little, the moon became thinner, until it was only a thin silver smile. Then, slowly, its light grew back, night after night, until it was round and bright again. And every month, the kind moon gives its light away once more." Why did the moon give away its light? Because some animals were afraid of the dark. What happened to the moon after that? It became thinner, and then it grew back. Riko says, That's a beautiful story. But what's the real reason? Ms. Emma smiles and explains. The moon doesn't really change shape. The sun always lights half of it. As the moon travels around the Earth, we see different amounts of that bright half. Sometimes we see all of it, and we call it a full moon. Sometimes we see only a thin crescent. One cycle takes about a month, about 29 and a half days. Nib claps his tiny paws. A true reason and a kind story. I like both! Now, phonics. Shiver means to shake because you are cold. Sparkle means to shine with many tiny flashes of light. Sparkle ends in l-e. Wander means to walk slowly with no special place to go. Next time, we'll visit a great hall full of stories from many lands. | Hello, everyone! | 1088 | `teacherScript` | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, Her hands were shaking. Today, there's a loud clang in the Story Workshop. Birimo has knocked over a pile of metal cookie tins! Nib laughs and helps him pick them up. Then he opens a new drawer. Inside, there are cards with animals on them. Nib says, When we compare one thing to another, it's called a simile. A simile puts a picture in the reader's head. Oren is about to do something very brave. We could just say he was brave. But it's more fun to say this. He was as brave as a lion. Say it with me. He was as brave as a lion. Great! Let's read the next page of The Frozen Kingdom. "Oren and his grandmother climbed higher and higher. The wind was as cold as ice. At the top of the mountain, they found an ice cave. Inside, the walls shone like glass, and there, in a pile of snow, lay the golden feathers. Suddenly, a huge white snake slid out and hissed. Oren's knees were shaking, but he didn't run. He was as brave as a lion. Excuse me, he said politely. We need those feathers to warm our kingdom. The snake blinked in surprise. Nobody has ever said excuse me to me, it said. Then it pushed the feathers toward him with its nose. Just at that moment, there was a whoosh, and a golden shape flew across the sky like a shooting star. It was the Sun Bird!" How was Oren brave? He didn't run, and he spoke politely to the snake. How did the Sun Bird fly? Like a shooting star. Riko says, I love the snake. It wasn't mean. It just needed someone to be polite. Nib nods and asks, And then what happened? Now, phonics. More sound words! Hiss is the sound a snake makes, or the sound of steam. Whoosh is the sound of something rushing past very fast, like the Sun Bird. Clang is the sound of metal hitting metal, like Birimo's cookie tins. Next time, we'll walk down a very noisy corridor. | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, Her hands were shaking. Today, there's a loud clang in the Story Workshop. Birimo has knocked over a pile of metal cookie tins! Nib laughs and helps him pick them up. Then he opens a new drawer. Inside, there are cards with animals on them. Nib says, When we compare one thing to another using as or like, it's called a simile. A simile puts a picture in the reader's head. Oren is about to do something very brave. We could just say he was brave. But it's more fun to say this. He was as brave as a lion. Say it with me. He was as brave as a lion. Great! Then it pushed the feathers toward him with its nose. Just at that moment, there was a whoosh, and a golden shape flew across the sky like a shooting star. It was the Sun Bird!" How was Oren brave? He didn't run, and he spoke politely to the snake. How did the Sun Bird fly? Like a shooting star. Riko says, I love the snake. It wasn't mean. It just needed someone to be polite. Nib nods and asks, And then what happened? Now, phonics. More sound words! Hiss is the sound a snake makes, or the sound of steam. Whoosh is the sound of something rushing past very fast, like the Sun Bird. Clang is the sound of metal hitting metal, like Birimo's cookie tins. | 1089 | `teacherScript` | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, He was as brave as a lion. Today, Nib leads us down the Creaking Corridor. It's long and narrow, and candles flicker on the walls. Every floorboard creaks under our feet, and our footsteps echo. Kiko holds Ms. Emma's hand. At the end, there is a tall wooden door. Nib grins and pushes it. Creak! The old door slowly opened. Say it with me. Creak! The old door slowly opened. Great! Behind the door, there is no monster at all, just Nib's sleepy cat, Button, yawning in a basket. Everyone laughs. Nib says, Sound words make a scene come alive. Let's read the last page of The Frozen Kingdom. "The Sun Bird landed on Oren's arm, and her feathers glowed. Together, Oren and his grandmother walked down the mountain, and the Sun Bird flew above them. When they reached the kingdom, she spread her golden wings. Crash! The thick ice on the river broke into pieces. Splash! The water began to run again, and the fish jumped for joy. Drip, drip, drip went the icicles on every roof. Then Oren knocked on the palace gate. Creak! The old door slowly opened, and the queen stepped out into the warm sunshine. Thank you, Oren, she said with a smile. From that day on, the Sun Bird kept her feathers safe, and every spring, the people held a festival of golden kites." What happened to the ice on the river? It broke into pieces. Who opened the palace door? The queen. Haru says, I liked the ending, because everyone was happy and the sheep were warm again. Riko asks, Did the snake come to the festival too? Nib laughs. That's a great question! And then what happened? You can decide! Now, phonics. Crash is the loud sound of something breaking or falling, like the ice on the river. Splash is the sound of something falling into water. Crash and splash both end in a-s-h. Creak is the sound of an old door or floor. Next time, it's the Story Lab, and each of you will read your own short paragraph! | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, He was as brave as a lion. Today, Nib leads us down the Creaking Corridor. Behind the door, there is no monster at all, just Nib's sleepy cat, Button, yawning in a basket. Everyone laughs. Nib says, Sound words make a scene come alive. | 1089 | `teacherScript` | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, He was as brave as a lion. Today, Nib leads us down the Creaking Corridor. It's long and narrow, and candles flicker on the walls. Every floorboard creaks under our feet, and our footsteps echo. Kiko holds my hand. At the end, there is a tall wooden door. Nib grins and pushes it. Creak! The old door slowly opened. Say it with me. Creak! The old door slowly opened. Great! Behind the door, there is no monster at all, just Nib's sleepy cat, Button, yawning in a basket. Everyone laughs. Nib says, Sound words make a scene come alive. Let's read the last page of The Frozen Kingdom. "The Sun Bird landed on Oren's arm, and her feathers glowed. Together, Oren and his grandmother walked down the mountain, and the Sun Bird flew above them. When they reached the kingdom, she spread her golden wings. Crash! The thick ice on the river broke into pieces. Splash! The water began to run again, and the fish jumped for joy. Drip, drip, drip went the icicles on every roof. Then Oren knocked on the palace gate. Creak! The old door slowly opened, and the queen stepped out into the warm sunshine. Thank you, Oren, she said with a smile. From that day on, the Sun Bird kept her feathers safe, and every spring, the people held a festival of golden kites." What happened to the ice on the river? It broke into pieces. Who opened the palace door? The queen. Haru says, I liked the ending, because everyone was happy and the sheep were warm again. Riko asks, Did the snake come to the festival too? Nib laughs. That's a great question! And then what happened? You can decide! Now, phonics. Crash is the loud sound of something breaking or falling, like the ice on the river. Splash is the sound of something falling into water. Crash and splash both end in a-s-h. Creak is the sound of an old door or floor. Next time, it's the Story Lab, and each of you will read your own short paragraph! | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, He was as brave as a lion. Today, Nib leads us down the Creaking Corridor. | 1091 | `teacherScript` | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, The forest was so quiet that she could hear her heartbeat. Today, Nib takes us down a new path in the Labyrinth. Ms. Emma walks at the front with a lantern, and we all stay close together. At the end of the path, there is a round room full of soft golden light. In the middle, on a pile of old cushions, lies a huge dragon. Her scales are gold, and her eyes are kind and sleepy. This is Saffron, and she is very old. Saffron smiles and says, Welcome, little travelers. Now my wings are tired, so I stay here and tell riddles. Kiko looks at Saffron's tired wings and says softly, If I were a dragon, I would fly over the ocean. Say it with me. If I were a dragon, I would fly over the ocean. Great! We say If I were ～ when we imagine something that isn't true. Let's read the riddle card on Saffron's wall. "Saffron's Riddle Room. Welcome, travelers! Long ago, I flew across the whole world. I saw seven seas, and I counted the stars over every one of them. Now my wings are old, but my mind is still young, so I love riddles. Here is today's riddle. I have pages, but I am not a tree. I have a spine, but I am not an animal. I can take you anywhere, but I have no legs. What am I? When you know the answer, tell me. Then tell me this: If you were a dragon, where would you fly? I will tell you a story about that place. Your friend, Saffron" Why does Saffron stay in the Dragon Room now? Because her wings are old and tired. What is the answer to the riddle? A book! Haru shouts it first, and Saffron's tail thumps happily on the floor. Now, phonics. When we tell a story aloud, we lower our voice and pause just before the surprise. She opened the box, and inside was, pause, a tiny dragon! The footsteps came closer and closer, and then, pause, they stopped. He looked up, and there in the tree sat, pause, a giant owl. Behind the curtain stood, pause, a little gray mouse. But never pause in the middle of a group like a tiny dragon! Next time, Saffron will show us a secret well that listens to wishes. | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, The forest was so quiet that she could hear her heartbeat. | 1092 | `teacherScript` | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, If I were a dragon, I would fly over the ocean. Today, Saffron shows us a small door behind her cushions. Ms. Emma goes first, and we follow her down some stone steps into a quiet garden. In the middle of the garden is an old stone well covered in moss. When Nib leans over the edge, the water bubbles, and a voice comes up. It sounds like a happy kettle! Blub, blub! Welcome to the Well of Wishes! The well doesn't want coins. It wants kind wishes, and you have to say them out loud. Birimo goes first. He says, I wish I could eat pancakes for breakfast forever! The water gives a little giggle. Then Kiko sees a bird on the edge of the well and says, I wish I could talk to animals. Say it with me. I wish I could talk to animals. Great! We say I wish I could ～ when we want to do something, but we can't do it now. Let's read the sign next to the well. "The Well of Wishes. Hello, traveler! I am a very old well, and I have heard thousands of wishes. Some were big, and some were small. Here are my three rules. First, say your wish out loud and clearly. I am a little hard of hearing! Second, make it a kind wish. Unkind wishes turn into mud, and nobody likes mud. Third, a wish is not a magic trick. If you wish you could swim, the best way is to practice. I will cheer for you! When a kind wish touches my water, it sparkles. Come closer, and let's see what happens. Blub, blub!" What does the well want instead of coins? Kind wishes, said out loud. What happens when a kind wish touches the water? It sparkles. When Kiko says her wish, the water sparkles like a thousand tiny stars, and the bird sings a little song. Now, phonics. Let's pause before the surprise again. The cave was dark and silent, until suddenly, pause, a voice said hello. At the bottom of the well, she found, pause, a golden key. The thing under the bed was not a monster, pause, but a puppy. When the clock struck twelve, the statue, pause, opened its eyes. Next time, Nib will teach us a question game with a magic key! | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, If I were a dragon, I would fly over the ocean. | 1094 | `teacherScript` | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, What would you do if you found a magic key? Today, Nib takes us to a small blue room. It smells like salt, and we can hear soft waves somewhere. On a shelf, there is a big pink seashell next to a pair of tiny hiking boots. Nib says, These belonged to Pell. Sit down, and I'll tell you her story. We sit around Ms. Emma on a soft rug. Pell lived in a village high in the mountains. She had seen snow, rivers and eagles, but she had never seen the sea before. Say it with me. She had never seen the sea before. Great! Had never seen（いちども 見た ことが なかった） means not even once, up until that time in the story. Let's read the page about Pell. "Pell lived in a village so high in the mountains that clouds floated through the streets. Every evening, her grandmother held a pink seashell to Pell's ear. Listen, she said. That is the sound of the sea. Pell had never seen the sea, but she dreamed about it every night. One spring, a kind traveling baker offered to take Pell and her grandmother down the mountain in his wagon. They traveled for seven days. The air got warmer, and the trees changed. On the last morning, Pell smelled something salty. She climbed to the top of a sandy hill, and she gasped. She had never seen the sea before, and now it was right in front of her, wide and blue and shining. She held up the shell. It sang the same song." Who traveled with Pell? Her grandmother and a kind baker. What did Pell smell on the last morning? Something salty. Kiko says quietly, The shell and the sea sang the same song. That's my favorite part. | 1095 | `teacherScript` | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, She had never seen the sea before. Today, we go back to the Dragon Room, because Saffron has a surprise. The Well of Wishes has sent a little stream of bubbly water up into a stone bowl next to her cushions. Saffron says, In many old stories, someone gets three wishes. Let's play. Tell me your three wishes, but remember, at least one of them must help other people. Riko goes first. If I had three wishes, I would ask for a flying bike, a library in my room and a sunny day for everyone. Say it with me. If I had three wishes, I would ask for a flying bike, a library in my room and a sunny day for everyone. Great! Riko's third wish helps everyone. Let's read the wish list that Nib wrote with his feather pen. "Our Three Wishes, by Nib. Riko wishes for a flying bike, a library in her room and a sunny day for everyone, so that nobody's picnic gets rained on. Haru wishes for a telescope that can see other planets, a robot that cleans his room and a big park for every town, where children and grandparents can play together. Kiko wishes she could talk to animals, of course! Her other wishes are a warm bed for every stray cat and a tree house in the clouds. Birimo wishes for pancakes forever, pancakes for his friends forever and, after a long think, a new pair of strong wings for Saffron. Saffron read the list twice and smiled. Then her golden eyes got a little wet." Which of Haru's wishes helps other people? A big park for every town. What was Birimo's last wish? New wings for Saffron. Saffron stretches her old wings and laughs softly. She says, That is the kindest wish I have heard in a hundred years. At that moment, the Storybook begins to glow. Nib opens it, and the third golden page appears, full of shining words. Nib reads it aloud. The third secret of stories: a wish is strongest when it is shared. Now, phonics. Let's review. She opened the box, and inside was, pause, a tiny dragon! Splash is the sound of something falling into water. Whisper starts with a w sound, and it means to speak very quietly, using your breath. Next time, we'll start planning our very own stories! | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, She had never seen the sea before. Today, we go back to the Dragon Room, because Saffron has a surprise. | 1096 | `teacherScript` | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, If I had three wishes, I would ask for a flying bike, a library in my room and a sunny day for everyone. Today, Nib takes us back to his Story Workshop. On the big table, there are four blank story maps. Each map has three boxes: HERO, PLACE and PROBLEM. Nib says, You've heard lots of stories in the Labyrinth. Now it's time to plan your own! Haru writes The Boy and the Stone Giant. Riko writes The Lighthouse Cat. Birimo writes The Dragon Who Sneezed Glitter. Kiko smiles and writes in the HERO box. My hero is a girl who can talk to the wind. Say it with me. My hero is a girl who can talk to the wind. Great! A hero is the main character of a story. Let's read Kiko's story map. "Story Map by Kiko. Title: The Girl Who Talked to the Wind. Hero: My hero is a girl named Aria who can talk to the wind. She is curious and kind, but sometimes she doesn't listen carefully. Place: Aria lives in a village above the clouds with her parents and her little brother. The wind visits her every evening and tells her about faraway lands. Problem: One night, a strange wind carries Aria far away from home. Now she is in a land she doesn't know, and she can't find the way back. Questions to think about: Who will help her? What will she learn? How will she get home? Nib's note: Great plan, Kiko! A hero with a small weakness is more interesting." What is Aria's weakness? Sometimes she doesn't listen carefully. What is her problem? A strange wind carries her far away, and she can't find the way back. Haru's hero is a boy named Leo who finds a sleepy stone giant on a mountain path. Riko's hero is a cat named Pepper who lives in an old lighthouse. Birimo's hero is a young dragon named Fizz who sneezes glitter instead of fire. Now, phonics. Words that end in -ly tell us how a character does something. Suddenly, the wind blew. She opened the door carefully. He walked bravely into the cave. To make these words, we usually just add -ly. Sudden becomes suddenly, careful becomes carefully, and brave becomes bravely. Next time, we'll learn how to write a first line that grabs the reader. | Hello, everyone! | 1097 | `teacherScript` | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, My hero is a girl who can talk to the wind. Today, Nib pulls open a drawer labeled BEGINNINGS. Inside, there are hundreds of tiny paper cards, and each card has the first line of a story. Nib says, The first line is like a door. If it opens in an exciting way, readers want to come in! Ms. Emma reads Kiko's first try out loud. Once there was a girl named Aria. She was nice. Kiko frowns. It's true, but it's a bit boring. Then she writes a new line. On the coldest night of the year, a strange wind knocked on her window. Say it with me. On the coldest night of the year, a strange wind knocked on her window. Great! Now we want to know who is knocking and why. Let's read Nib's workshop card. "Nib's Workshop Card: Strong Beginnings. A strong beginning makes the reader ask a question. Here are two beginnings of the same story. Beginning A: Once there was a girl named Aria. She lived above the clouds. She was nice. Beginning B: On the coldest night of the year, a strange wind knocked on her window. Aria sat up in bed. The wind had never knocked before. Beginning A gives us facts, but nothing happens. Beginning B starts with a surprise, so we want to read more. Try these tricks. Start with a sound. Start with something strange. Start with a question, like Who would knock on a window at midnight? Then ask yourself: Would I turn the page?" Why is Beginning B stronger? It starts with a surprise, so we want to read more. What is one trick for a strong beginning? Start with a sound, something strange or a question. Riko writes, On a stormy night, Pepper the lighthouse cat could not sleep. Birimo's first line is just one word. Achoo! Nib laughs and says, That's a sound, so it counts! Now, phonics. The cat crept silently down the stairs. Aria nervously opened the window. The wind gently touched her face. Silent becomes silently. Nervous becomes nervously. Gentle is a little different: we take away the e and add y, so it becomes gently. Next time, we'll find the moment in our stories where everything changes. | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, My hero is a girl who can talk to the wind. | 1097 | `teacherScript` | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, My hero is a girl who can talk to the wind. Today, Nib pulls open a drawer labeled BEGINNINGS. Inside, there are hundreds of tiny paper cards, and each card has the first line of a story. Nib says, The first line is like a door. If it opens in an exciting way, readers want to come in! I read Kiko's first try out loud. Once there was a girl named Aria. She was nice. Kiko frowns. It's true, but it's a bit boring. Then she writes a new line. On the coldest night of the year, a strange wind knocked on her window. Say it with me. On the coldest night of the year, a strange wind knocked on her window. Great! Now we want to know who is knocking and why. Let's read Nib's workshop card. "Nib's Workshop Card: Strong Beginnings. A strong beginning makes the reader ask a question. Here are two beginnings of the same story. Beginning A: Once there was a girl named Aria. She lived above the clouds. She was nice. Beginning B: On the coldest night of the year, a strange wind knocked on her window. Aria sat up in bed. The wind had never knocked before. Beginning A gives us facts, but nothing happens. Beginning B starts with a surprise, so we want to read more. Try these tricks. Start with a sound. Start with something strange. Start with a question, like Who would knock on a window at midnight? Then ask yourself: Would I turn the page?" Why is Beginning B stronger? It starts with a surprise, so we want to read more. What is one trick for a strong beginning? | 1098 | `teacherScript` | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, On the coldest night of the year, a strange wind knocked on her window. Today, Nib draws a big hill on the blackboard in the Story Workshop. At the bottom of one side, he writes BEGINNING. At the top, he writes TURNING POINT. At the bottom of the other side, he writes ENDING. He says, A story is like climbing a hill. At the top, something happens that changes everything. That's the turning point! Ms. Emma asks, What's the turning point in Pell's story? Riko answers, When she sees the sea! Exactly. Now Kiko needs a turning point for Aria. The strange wind has carried Aria to a land of dry deserts and tall mountains, and she is lost and tired. Kiko writes, Just then, she heard a voice behind her. Say it with me. Just then, she heard a voice behind her. Great! Just then means at that exact moment. Let's read Kiko's middle page. "Aria walked and walked across the dry sand. Everything was silent. She called the wind's name, but nobody answered. Her feet were tired, and she missed her little brother. She sat down on a rock and hugged her knees. Just then, she heard a voice behind her. It was quiet and a little shy. Excuse me, it said. Are you the girl who talks to the wind? Aria turned around slowly. There, floating in the air, was a tiny breeze, no bigger than a kitten. I'm the youngest wind, it said, and I'm the one who carried you here. I'm sorry. I got lost, and I needed a friend who could listen." How did Aria feel before the turning point? Tired, and she missed her little brother. Who was talking behind her? The youngest wind. Birimo shouts, No way! The wind was the problem and the friend! Nib grins and says, And then what happened? You'll find out in the next chapter! Haru's turning point is when the stone giant opens his eyes and says, Please don't go. Riko's is when the cat sees a little boat lost in the fog. Now, phonics. The stone giant stood up proudly. The cat eagerly ran up the stairs. Outside, the wind blew fiercely. Proud becomes proudly. Eager becomes eagerly. Fierce becomes fiercely: we keep the e and add -ly. Next time, we'll learn how to give our stories a happy, satisfying ending. | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, On the coldest night of the year, a strange wind knocked on her window. | 1099 | `teacherScript` | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, Just then, she heard a voice behind her. Today, Nib points to the other side of the hill on his blackboard, the side that goes down to the ENDING. He says, A good ending does two things. It solves the problem, and it shows how the hero has changed. Ms. Emma adds, And it should feel just right, like the last piece of a puzzle. So what does Aria learn? At the beginning, she didn't always listen carefully. So in the ending, she listens. She hears that the little wind is lonely, and she becomes its friend. Then Kiko writes her last line. In the end, the wind carried her home. Say it with me. In the end, the wind carried her home. Great! In the end means finally, after everything that happened. Let's read Kiko's last page. "Aria listened to the youngest wind for a long time. It told her about the places it had seen and how lonely it felt, because the big winds were always too busy to play. I can be your friend, Aria said, but my family will be worried. Can you take me home? The little wind thought for a moment. Then it blew softly, and Aria rose into the air, as light as a dandelion seed. They flew over deserts, rivers and sleeping towns, up to her village. In the end, the wind carried her home. Every evening after that, a small breeze tapped on her window, and Aria always opened it to listen." Why was the youngest wind lonely? Because the big winds were always too busy to play. How does Aria change? She learns to listen carefully. Riko says, That's such a satisfying ending. Haru's story ends with the stone giant waving to travelers on the mountain path. In Riko's story, the cat lights the lamp, and the little boat gets home safely. In Birimo's story, the king loves the glitter so much that he holds a Sparkle Festival every year. Nib says, And then what happened? Nothing! That's how you know it's the end! Now, phonics. The boat finally reached the harbor. The giant looked curiously at the boy. The dragon danced gracefully in the sky. Final becomes finally. Curious becomes curiously. Graceful becomes gracefully, just like careful and carefully. Next time is Lesson 1100, and Nib says there will be a party! | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, Just then, she heard a voice behind her. | 1100 | `teacherScript` | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, In the end, the wind carried her home. Today is a very special day. It's our 1,100th lesson! When we walk into the Story Workshop, we all gasp. The room is full of paper lanterns, and each lantern is folded from a page of an old story. On the big table, there is a tall cake shaped like a stack of books, with a tiny chocolate mouse on top. Ms. Emma lights the candles. Saffron has sent a basket of warm cinnamon rolls from the Dragon Room, and the Well of Wishes has sent a big bottle of bubbly lemonade. Nib climbs onto a teacup and shouts, Let's share our story ideas! Say it with me. Let's share our story ideas! Great! Let's read the party program. "The Story Idea Party! Program. One: Cake and lemonade. Nib asks you to save the chocolate mouse for him. Two: Story Idea Share. Each friend tells the title, the hero and the problem of their story in one minute. Kiko: The Girl Who Talked to the Wind. Riko: The Lighthouse Cat. Haru: The Boy and the Stone Giant. Birimo: The Dragon Who Sneezed Glitter. Three: Friendly Questions. After each idea, ask one question, like Who helps the hero? or What happens next? Four: Lantern Wishes. Write one kind wish for your story on a paper lantern. Please clap loudly and laugh kindly. Thank you for being such brave and creative storytellers!" What does each friend tell in one minute? The title, the hero and the problem of their story. What do the friends write on the paper lanterns? One kind wish for their story. The friends share their ideas bravely, and everyone claps. When Birimo shouts Achoo, the whole room laughs. Then the Storybook begins to glow, and the fourth golden page appears! It says, The fourth secret of stories: every story begins with one small idea. Far away in the maze, we hear an old voice telling a story by a crackling fire. Now, phonics. Let's review. Bravely means in a brave way: brave plus -ly. At the bottom of the well, she found, pause, a golden key. Boom is the sound of fireworks or thunder. Glimmer means to shine with a weak light that comes and goes, just like our paper lanterns. Next time, we'll meet Granny Willa, the old storyteller in the Hearth Room! | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, In the end, the wind carried her home. Today is a very special day. It's our 1,100th lesson! When we walk into the Story Workshop, we all gasp. The room is full of paper lanterns, and each lantern is folded from a page of an old story. On the big table, there is a tall cake shaped like a stack of books, with a tiny chocolate mouse on top. I light the candles. Nib climbs onto a teacup and shouts, Let's share our story ideas! Say it with me. Let's share our story ideas! Great! Let's read the party program. "The Story Idea Party! Program. | 1103 | `teacherScript` | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, Can you guess what happened next? Today, Birimo is so excited that he can't sit still. He stands in front of the fire, holds up his notebook and reads his story to all of us. When he finishes, Granny Willa puts down her knitting and says, Now, my dears, a storyteller grows when friends give kind feedback. Here is my rule. First, say something you liked, and be specific. Then, give one gentle idea, not ten. Use Maybe you could, so it sounds friendly. Haru goes first. He says, I loved the part where the dragon sneezed. Maybe you could tell us more about the castle. Say it with me. I loved the part where the dragon sneezed. Maybe you could tell us more about the castle. Great! Birimo grins from ear to ear. Let's read the story that Birimo read to us. "The Dragon Who Sneezed Glitter, by Birimo. Fizz was a small green dragon who lived on a hill next to a big castle. Fizz had a problem. Whenever he smelled flowers, his nose began to tickle. Ah... ah... ACHOO! Instead of fire, a cloud of glitter came out! It flew across the sky like a shooting star and landed on everything. The queen's cat turned pink and sparkly. The guards' helmets turned gold and purple. Even the castle, which had been as white as snow, was now every color of the rainbow. Fizz felt terrible, so he hid behind a big rock. But the next morning, people came from far away to see the shining castle. The queen laughed and told Fizz, You're the best decorator in the kingdom!" What came out when Fizz sneezed? A cloud of glitter. Why did people come from far away? To see the shining castle. Kiko says, I loved the part where the cat turned pink. Maybe you could add a sound word for the glitter. Birimo nods. Thanks! That's a great idea. Granny Willa smiles. You see? Kind words first, then one idea. Now, phonics. Here are three more similes. The castle was as white as snow. Saffron's old scales are as hard as a rock. A simile can use like, too. The glitter flew like a shooting star. Which word goes in as white as ... ? Snow! Next time, we'll use our friends' ideas to rewrite our stories. | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, Can you guess what happened next? Today, Birimo is so excited that he can't sit still. | 1104 | `teacherScript` | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, I loved the part where the dragon sneezed. Maybe you could tell us more about the castle. Today, Granny Willa gives each of us a fresh sheet of paper and a pencil. She says, The first draft is never the last draft, my dears. Even I rewrite my stories again and again. Then she shows us three tricks. One: change plain words into vivid words. Instead of said, try whispered, shouted or muttered. Two: add a simile, so the listener can see the picture. Three: cut extra words that don't help the story. Riko works on The Lighthouse Cat. When she finishes, she holds up her paper proudly and says, I changed 'said' to 'whispered.' Say it with me. I changed 'said' to 'whispered.' Great! She changed a lot of other things, too. Let's read the two versions of Riko's paragraph. "Before: The cat was in the lighthouse. It was night. There was a storm. A boat was coming. The cat was scared. She said, I have to help. She went up the stairs. She turned on the light. The boat was safe. After: On a stormy night, Pepper the lighthouse cat could not sleep. Far out at sea, a little fishing boat was rocking on the waves, and the lighthouse lamp had gone out! Her tail was shaking, but she whispered, I have to help. She ran like the wind up two hundred stairs and pushed the big switch with both paws. Click! The light swept across the water, as bright as the moon. Slowly, the little boat turned toward the harbor. That night, Pepper slept like a log." How did Riko show that Pepper was scared? Her tail was shaking. What did Pepper do to help the boat? She ran up the stairs and turned on the light. Haru says, Your new version is much better! It feels like a movie. Riko smiles. Thanks for your ideas yesterday. They really helped. Now, phonics. These similes use like. After a long, busy day, we sleep like a log. A good swimmer can swim like a fish. And Pepper ran like the wind. Which word goes in sleep like a ... ? Log! Next time, we'll tell our stories by the fire on Storytelling Night. | Hello, everyone! | 1105 | `teacherScript` | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, I changed 'said' to 'whispered.' Tonight is Storytelling Night in the Hearth Room! Nib has put cushions and soft blankets all around the fire, and Granny Willa hands everyone a warm cup of cocoa. Ms. Emma sits with us in the firelight. Even Saffron, the old golden dragon, has come to listen. Each friend tells the beginning of a story. Riko tells The Lighthouse Cat, and Haru tells The Boy and the Stone Giant in a big, deep voice. Birimo tells The Dragon Who Sneezed Glitter, with a new sound word, whoosh! Then it's Kiko's turn. She takes a deep breath, pauses, and begins softly. Once, in a land beyond the clouds, there lived a girl who could talk to the wind. Say it with me. Once, in a land beyond the clouds, there lived a girl who could talk to the wind. Beautiful! Let's read the first page of Kiko's story. "Once, in a land beyond the clouds, there lived a girl who could talk to the wind. Her name was Aria. Every morning, the wind told her secrets: where the birds were flying, which flowers were opening, and when the rain was coming. Aria loved her village in the sky, with its cloud gardens and rainbow bridges. On the coldest night of the year, a strange wind knocked on her window. Its voice was soft and a little sad, and it was singing a song that Aria had never heard before. Come with me, it said. I'm lost, and I need a friend who can listen. Aria pulled on her warm coat and opened the window. The wind lifted her up, gently but quickly, over the edge of the clouds, toward a land she had never seen." Who is the hero of Kiko's story? A girl named Aria who can talk to the wind. Why did the strange wind come? Because it was lost and needed a friend. Everyone claps. Saffron's eyes shine. Then the Storybook starts to glow, and a fifth golden page appears! Nib reads it aloud. The fifth secret of stories: a story comes alive when you tell it aloud. Now, phonics. Let's review. He was as brave as a lion. After a long wait, finally, it was Kiko's turn. Out of the giant egg came, pause, a baby star. Leaves flutter in the wind. | 1106 | `teacherScript` | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, Once, in a land beyond the clouds, there lived a girl who could talk to the wind. Nib leads us to a quiet reading room full of soft chairs. On a tall stand, there is one thick book. Nib taps the cover with his feather pen and says, This tale is longer than the others, so read slowly. Ms. Emma asks one big question. Read the tale. Why did the king change his mind? Say it with me. Read the tale. Why did the king change his mind? Great! Let's read it together. "The King Who Banned Singing. Long ago, in a busy little town, there lived a king who was always grumpy. Every day, the town was full of noise. Bakers sang as they baked, children sang on their way to the market, and the bells rang every hour. The noise gave the king terrible headaches. So one morning, he made a new rule: No more singing! The town became very quiet, and very sad. Then one stormy night, thunder rolled across the sky. In her family's barn near the castle, a baby goat was so frightened that it couldn't stop crying. A little girl named Tess sat down beside it with her lantern and gently hummed a soft song. Slowly, the baby goat closed its eyes. Up in his tower, the king heard the song through his window. For the first time in years, he felt peaceful. The next morning, he changed the rule. Soft songs were allowed again, and from then on, the town had a singing day every year." Why did the king ban singing? Because the noise gave him headaches. Why did he change his mind? Because Tess's soft song made him feel peaceful for the first time in years. Kiko says, I think the king wasn't really bad. He was just tired. Riko says, I see your point, but he made the whole town sad. Nib claps his tiny paws. Good readers can disagree, as long as they give reasons! Now, phonics. Let's review. Glance means look very quickly. Sizzle is the sound of food in a hot pan. She had never seen the sea before, and now, pause, it was right in front of her. Tess gently hummed a song. The stormy night was as cold as ice. Next time, we'll hear a story told by a wolf. | Hello, everyone! | 1108 | `teacherScript` | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, What if the story were told by the wolf? Today is a big day. Nib takes us to a sunny writing room with long wooden desks, colored pencils and stacks of clean paper. On each desk, there is a blank book cover. Nib says, Today, you finish your stories and make your covers. A story isn't finished until it has a title! Ms. Emma walks around the room and helps everyone. Haru draws a huge stone giant with a gentle smile. Riko draws a cat on top of a lighthouse. Birimo decorates his cover with real glitter, and now his nose is sparkly, too. Kiko writes her title in curly blue letters, and then she reads it to us proudly. My story is called The Girl Who Talked to the Wind. Say it with me. My story is called The Girl Who Talked to the Wind. Wonderful! Let's read what Kiko wrote on the back cover of her book. "The Girl Who Talked to the Wind, by Kiko. Aria lives in a village above the clouds, and she can talk to the wind. On the coldest night of the year, a strange wind knocks on her window and carries her far away, to a land of dry deserts and tall mountains. Aria is lost and lonely. Just then, she hears a voice behind her. It's the youngest wind, and it is lost and lonely, too! Aria listens to its story and helps it find its way, and the two become good friends. In the end, the wind carries her home, and every night after that, a warm breeze sings outside her window. A story about friendship, courage and listening. For readers who love adventure and the sky." Whose voice does Aria hear behind her? The youngest wind's. How does Aria get home? The wind carries her home. Riko says, I love the last line. It feels so cozy. Kiko smiles. I'm proud of my ending. It's warm and happy. Now, phonics. Let's review. Kiko gives a big grin when she finishes. Drip is the sound of water falling one drop at a time. He reached into his pocket and pulled out, pause, a glowing stone. Kiko read her title proudly. After all that writing, tonight we'll sleep like a log. Next time, we'll take a goodbye tour of the Labyrinth. | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, What if the story were told by the wolf? Today is a big day. | 1109 | `teacherScript` | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, My story is called The Girl Who Talked to the Wind. Today, Nib takes us on a goodbye tour of the Labyrinth. He holds the Storybook under his arm, and we walk past every room we have visited. We wave to the clever rabbit's room and the Moon Room. Saffron lifts her golden head from her pile of old books and says, Here's one last riddle for you. What has a beginning, a middle and an end, and lives inside you? A story! In the Hearth Room, Granny Willa gives each of us a warm hug. Then Riko says, I've learned so much here. If I were a storyteller, I would tell stories as bright as the stars. Say it with me. If I were a storyteller, I would tell stories as bright as the stars. Lovely! Let's read Nib's map of our journey. "Our Journey Through the Labyrinth of Tales. In the first rooms, we heard tales from many lands: a clever rabbit, a moon that shares its light, and brave children everywhere. In Nib's Story Workshop, we learned about setting, characters and problems. We learned to show, not tell, and to use similes and sound words. In the Dragon Room and at the Well of Wishes, we imagined what we would do with magic. Then we planned our own stories, with strong beginnings, exciting turning points and happy endings. In the Hearth Room, Granny Willa taught us to tell stories aloud and to give kind feedback. Now our stories are finished, and the Storytelling Festival is waiting in the Central Hall!" Where did we learn about setting, characters and problems? In Nib's Story Workshop. Who taught us to tell stories aloud? Granny Willa. At the very end of the tour, we come to the exit of the maze. There is a round window in the wall. Through it, we can see rolling green hills under a soft sky, and the wind smells of flowers. Kiko asks, What's that place? Nib smiles. That's the Highlands of the Heart. Now, phonics. Let's review. Stumble means almost fall while walking. Whoosh is the sound of something rushing past. The thing under the bed was not a monster, pause, but a puppy. Haru waited nervously for his turn. In the library, we were as quiet as a mouse. | 1110 | `teacherScript` | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, If I were a storyteller, I would tell stories as bright as the stars. Today is the Storytelling Festival! The Central Hall is at the very heart of the Labyrinth. It is round and tall, with shelves that go up and up, and thousands of tiny lanterns glimmer above us. Everyone is here. Granny Willa sits in the front row in her patchwork shawl. Saffron lies along the back wall, and the clever rabbit sits on her tail. Ms. Emma sits with us, and Nib stands on the stage in his red cape. Haru tells part of The Boy and the Stone Giant. Riko tells part of The Lighthouse Cat. Birimo tells The Dragon Who Sneezed Glitter, and everyone laughs. Kiko tells the end of her story, and the hall goes quiet. Then Nib says, Every one of us has a story to tell. Say it with me. Every one of us has a story to tell. Wonderful! Let's read the festival program. "The Storytelling Festival of the Labyrinth of Tales. Welcome to the Central Hall! Today, four young storytellers will share the stories they wrote here. Haru: The Boy and the Stone Giant, a tale about a lonely giant who learns to laugh. Riko: The Lighthouse Cat, a tale about a small cat with a big heart. Birimo: The Dragon Who Sneezed Glitter, a very funny tale. Please don't sit too close! Kiko: The Girl Who Talked to the Wind, a tale about friendship and listening. Please listen quietly, and cheer at the end of each story. When the last story is told, keep your eyes on the Storybook." Who tells the funny tale? Birimo. What should the audience do at the end of each story? Cheer. The cheers are loud and happy. Then the sixth golden page appears: The sixth secret of stories: every one of us has a story to tell. The Storybook is complete! It glows like a little sun. As Nib closes it, a pressed purple flower falls out with a note. It says, Come to the Highlands of the Heart. We remember the green hills from the window. Our next adventure is waiting! Now, phonics. Let's review. Lanterns glimmer in the dark. Old doors creak. The voice in the dark whispered one word, pause, welcome. Kiko told her story bravely. The idea came to her like a shooting star. Goodbye, Nib! Thank you, Granny Willa! Thank you, Labyrinth of Tales! | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, If I were a storyteller, I would tell stories as bright as the stars. | 1111 | `teacherScript` | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, Every one of us has a story to tell. Nib walks with us to the round exit of the Labyrinth, holding the Storybook. He waves goodbye with his little paw. Then we step outside, and wow! Green hills roll away in every direction. There are old stone walls, fluffy white sheep and fields of purple heather. Down in the valley is a little village called Heatherbrook. On the highest hill, Heart Hill, a huge rainbow hangs in the sky. The rainbow is pale and white, with no colors at all. Suddenly, a small, soft cloud floats down next to Kiko. Hello! I'm Puff, it says. Welcome to the Highlands of the Heart. How are you really feeling? Haru laughs, and the sunshine gets a little warmer. Puff explains, The weather here changes with your feelings. Say it with me. The weather here changes with your feelings. Great! Then a kind old woman in a woolly hat comes up the path with a sheepdog. She is Grandma Rowan, a shepherd, and her dog is called Biscuit. You can all stay at my farmhouse with your teacher, she says. Let's read the sign on her gate. "Welcome to the Highlands of the Heart! Here, the sky listens to your heart. When you are sad, a small rain cloud may follow you. When you feel shy or embarrassed, a pink mist may appear around you. When friends laugh together, warm sunshine pours down. And when people argue, thick fog rolls over the hills. Please don't worry. All feelings are welcome here. Long ago, people in the highlands shared their feelings every day, and the Great Rainbow above Heart Hill was full of color. Then people stopped talking about their hearts, and the colors faded. If you learn the skills of the heart, the rainbow will shine again." What happens when friends laugh together? Warm sunshine pours down. Why did the colors fade? Because people stopped talking about their hearts. Puff turns a little gray. Each time you learn a heart skill, Puff says, a color will return to the rainbow. We all promise to help. Now, phonics. Some feeling words have tricky spellings. Embarrassed has two r's and two s's. Frustrated is fru, s, tra, ted, with no extra e. Relieved has i before e. Next time, we'll find better words than just happy. | Hello, everyone! | 1113 | `teacher.segments[0].subtitleJa` | みんな、やあ！ まえの かいは「ただ うれしい だけ じゃ ない。もう さいこうに しあわせ！」と いったね。でも きょうは、たいへん！ ビスケットが ビリモの くつしたを くわえてる！ | やあ、みんな！ まえの かいは「ただ うれしい だけ じゃ ない。もう さいこうに しあわせ！」と いったね。でも きょうは、たいへん！ ビスケットが ビリモの くつしたを くわえてる！ | A+C (block rule; group report L1121-1130) | JA opening greeting unified with the block-wide fix: みんな、やあ！ -> やあ、みんな！ (more natural word order) |
| 1114 | `teacherScript` | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, I'm a little annoyed, not angry. Today, Ms. Emma takes us down the hill to the village of Heatherbrook. The village green is busy. Some village children are playing ball near the old well. They wave at us, and Riko waves back with a big smile. But the path is wet after last night's rain. Riko steps on a slippery patch, her feet slide out, and splat! She sits down right in a puddle of mud. Riko starts to blush, and a soft pink mist appears all around her. She feels embarrassed（はずかしい）. Then a girl from the village giggles, and a boy calls out, Don't worry! That puddle catches everybody! I fell in it last week! The children laugh, but it's a kind laugh, and Riko starts laughing too. The girl helps her up and lends her a towel. The pink mist floats away, and the sun comes out. Later, Puff asks Riko how she really felt. Riko says, I was so embarrassed, but then I felt relieved. Say it with me. I was so embarrassed, but then I felt relieved. Lovely! That evening, Riko writes in her diary. Let's read it. "Dear Diary, today I fell into a muddy puddle in front of all the village kids. At first, I wanted to hide under a rock. My face was hot, and a pink mist was all around me. Then a boy told me that the puddle catches everybody, and everyone laughed together. They weren't laughing at me. They were laughing with me. A girl helped me up, and she even lent me her towel. I felt so relieved! My heart went from stormy to sunny in one minute. Now I have some new friends in Heatherbrook, and I learned something important. Everybody makes mistakes, and a kind laugh can turn an embarrassing moment into a happy memory." What did the boy say about the puddle? It catches everybody. How did Riko feel at the end? Relieved. Puff turns a happy gold. Now, phonics. Furious ends with i, o, u, s. Lonely is lone plus ly, so keep the e in the middle. Exhausted has a silent h after the x. After playing in the village all afternoon, Haru flops down on the grass and says, I'm exhausted! Next time, each of us will give a feelings weather report. | Hello, everyone! | 1115 | `teacherScript` | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, I was so embarrassed, but then I felt relieved. Today, Grandma Rowan has an idea. Every evening, she listens to the weather report on her old radio. Why don't you make your own weather report, she says, a feelings weather report? Puff is so excited that the little cloud turns bright gold. We sit by the fire, and one by one, we stand up like real weather reporters. Kiko goes first. She's a little anxious about the village welcome party next week, but she's grateful for her new friends. So she says, Today, my feelings are cloudy with some sunshine. Say it with me. Today, my feelings are cloudy with some sunshine. Excellent! Next, it's Haru's turn. Let's read his weather report. "Good evening, everyone! Here is Haru's feelings weather report. This morning started bright and sunny, because we had Grandma Rowan's pancakes for breakfast. Around noon, there was a short shower of rain. I couldn't find my hat, and I was a bit frustrated. Luckily, Riko found it under the bench, so the rain stopped quickly. In the afternoon, there were strong winds of excitement, because we explored the stone walls and counted forty sheep. Tonight, I'm feeling warm and calm, with a light breeze of sleepiness. Tomorrow's forecast: sunny skies and lots of laughter, with a small chance of more pancakes. That's all from me. Back to you, Puff!" Why was Haru frustrated around noon? He couldn't find his hat. What's the forecast for tomorrow? Sunny skies and lots of laughter. Then Birimo gives his report. Tonight, it's sunny and sticky, with a one hundred percent chance of syrup! We laugh so hard that warm sunshine fills the room, even though it's night outside. Suddenly, Puff zooms to the window. Look at Heart Hill! A soft red light is glowing in the Great Rainbow. The red stripe came back! We learned to name our feelings, and red returned to the rainbow. Grandma Rowan wipes away a happy tear, and Biscuit wags his tail. Now, phonics. Let's review. Embarrassed has a double r and a double s. Anxious means a little worried, and it's spelled with an x and i, o, u, s. Grateful is grate plus ful. Next time, Kiko will tell us about a problem with her flute. | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, I was so embarrassed, but then I felt relieved. Today, Grandma Rowan has an idea. Every evening, she listens to the weather report on her old radio. Why don't you make your own weather report, she says, a feelings weather report? Puff is so excited that the little cloud turns bright gold. We sit by the fire, and one by one, we stand up like real weather reporters. Kiko goes first. She's a little anxious about the village welcome party next week, but she's grateful for her new friends. So she says, Today, my feelings are cloudy with some sunshine. Say it with me. Today, my feelings are cloudy with some sunshine. Excellent! Next, it's Haru's turn. Let's read his weather report. | 1116 | `teacherScript` | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, Today, my feelings are cloudy with some sunshine. Next Saturday, Heatherbrook will have a welcome party for us on the village green, and Kiko has been asked to play a highland tune on her flute. She practices every afternoon behind Grandma Rowan's farmhouse. But today, the high notes keep squeaking. Squee! Squee! Kiko stops, puts her flute in her lap and stares at her shoes. A little gray rain cloud appears right above her. Riko sits down next to her and holds an umbrella over her. What's wrong? Kiko says, My flute keeps squeaking. I've practiced so much, and it's still not right. Riko doesn't laugh, and she doesn't give advice. She just says softly, That must be hard. I'm sorry to hear that. Say it with me. That must be hard. I'm sorry to hear that. Very kind! Puff floats close and turns a gentle gray, too. Then Puff opens the Feelings Book to the advice corner. Let's read it. "Dear Puff, I have a problem. I'm learning to ride a bike, but I keep falling off. My big sister rides so easily. I feel sad, and I want to give up. From, Wobbly Wheels. Dear Wobbly Wheels, thank you for telling me how you feel. That must be hard. Learning something new can be really frustrating, especially when other people make it look easy. Remember, your sister was a beginner once, too. Every wobble means you are trying. Try practicing for ten minutes a day on soft grass. And if something ever feels really hard for a long time, talk to a grown-up you trust, like someone in your family or a teacher. Your friend, Puff." What is Wobbly Wheels learning to do? Ride a bike. Who can you talk to when something feels really hard for a long time? A grown-up you trust, like a family member or a teacher. Kiko smiles a tiny smile, and the rain cloud gets a little smaller. Now, phonics. These are phrasal verbs, two small words that work together. Cheer up means feel happier. Link the sounds, and cheer up sounds like chee-rup. Calm down means become quiet and peaceful again. Make up means become friends again after a fight. Say the second word strongly: cheer UP, calm DOWN, make UP. Next time, Haru will tell Kiko a story about his piano. | Hello, everyone! I've practiced so much, and it's still not right. Riko doesn't laugh, and she doesn't give advice. She just says softly, That must be hard. I'm sorry to hear that. Say it with me. That must be hard. | 1118 | `teacherScript` | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, I know how you feel. I had the same problem. Kiko is practicing with a smile now. But today, someone else has a cloud over his head. It's Birimo! Grandma Rowan is teaching him to knit, because he wants to make a scarf for Puff. The problem is that his knitting keeps coming apart. The yarn gets tangled, the stitches slide off the needles, and his scarf has more holes than scarf. Birimo groans. A small cloud starts to rumble above him. Riko wants to help. But first, she asks a question. Do you want advice, or do you just want me to listen? Say it with me. Do you want advice, or do you just want me to listen? Great! Birimo thinks for a second and says, I just want you to listen, please. Riko listens and nods. After a few minutes, Birimo sighs. Thanks. I feel better. Now I'd like some advice! Let's read Puff's Feelings Book. "Two Ways to Help a Friend. When a friend is upset, we often want to fix the problem right away. We give advice, like Try this, or Don't worry. Sometimes that helps a lot. But sometimes a friend doesn't want ideas yet. They just want someone to hear how they feel. So before you help, ask, Do you want advice, or do you just want me to listen? If your friend wants you to listen, look at them, nod and let them finish. If they want advice, share one or two ideas kindly. And remember, if a problem feels too big for friends, it's a good idea to talk to a grown-up you trust, like a family member or a teacher." What should you ask before you help? Do you want advice, or do you just want me to listen? What can you do when a friend wants you to listen? Look at them, nod and let them finish. Grandma Rowan smiles and shows Birimo how to hold the needles. Slowly, the scarf begins to grow. Now, phonics. Open up means start to talk about your feelings. Birimo opened up to Riko. Count on means trust someone to help you. Stand up for means support someone when they need it. Link open up and count on, and say the little words strongly. Next time, it's the welcome party, and Kiko will play her flute! | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, I know how you feel. I had the same problem. Kiko is practicing with a smile now. But first, she asks a question. Do you want advice, or do you just want me to listen? Say it with me. Do you want advice, or do you just want me to listen? Great! Birimo thinks for a second and says, I just want you to listen, please. Riko listens and nods. After a few minutes, Birimo sighs. Thanks. I feel better. Now I'd like some advice! Let's read Puff's Feelings Book. "Two Ways to Help a Friend. When a friend is upset, we often want to fix the problem right away. We give advice, like Try this, or Don't worry. Sometimes that helps a lot. But sometimes a friend doesn't want ideas yet. They just want someone to hear how they feel. So before you help, ask, Do you want advice, or do you just want me to listen? If your friend wants you to listen, look at them, nod and let them finish. If they want advice, share one or two ideas kindly. And remember, if a problem feels too big for friends, it's a good idea to talk to a grown-up you trust, like a family member or a teacher." What should you ask before you help? Do you want advice, or do you just want me to listen? What can you do when a friend wants you to listen? Look at them, nod and let them finish. Grandma Rowan smiles and shows Birimo how to hold the needles. Slowly, the scarf begins to grow. Now, phonics. Open up means start to talk about your feelings. Birimo opened up to Riko. Count on means trust someone to help you. Stand up for means support someone when they need it. Link open up and count on. Say UP strongly in open up, but COUNT strongly in count on. | 1120 | `teacherScript` | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, Cheer up! You'll do better next time. The morning after the party, Ms. Emma has a plan. You've learned so much about listening, she says. Let's practice being good listeners with a role-play. One person tells a small problem, and the other person listens. Riko and Kiko go first. Riko pretends that her painting of the purple heather got muddy, because Biscuit walked right across it. Kiko looks at her kindly, nods and says, Tell me what happened. Say it with me. Tell me what happened. Perfect! Grandma Rowan pins a poster on the kitchen wall. Let's read it. "How to Be a Good Listener. Step one: Stop what you are doing, and look at your friend kindly. Step two: Invite them to talk. You can say, Tell me what happened. Step three: Listen to the whole story. Nod, and don't interrupt, even if you have a great idea. Step four: Ask gentle questions, like Then what happened? or How do you feel now? Step five: Show that you understand. Say, That must be hard, or I know how you feel. Step six: Ask, Do you want advice, or do you just want me to listen? Good listeners don't have to fix everything. Sometimes a friend just needs a warm, quiet place to calm down. And if a problem is very big, help your friend find a grown-up they can count on." What can you say to invite a friend to talk? Tell me what happened. What should you do even if you have a great idea? Don't interrupt. Riko tells the whole story, and Kiko listens without interrupting once. At the end, Kiko asks, How do you feel now? Riko says, I feel calmer. Thanks for listening. Ms. Emma reminds us that if something is really hard, we can always talk to a grown-up we trust, like a family member or a teacher. Suddenly, the kitchen glows. Puff zooms to the window. Look! A warm orange light is spreading across the Great Rainbow. Orange returned to the rainbow! We learned to listen with our hearts. Now, phonics. Let's review. Calm down means become peaceful again. Count on means trust someone to help you. Relieved has i before e. Next time, we'll walk toward Heart Hill with a map, but Haru and Riko won't agree about the way. | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, Cheer up! You'll do better next time. Sometimes a friend just needs a warm, quiet place to calm down. And if a problem is very big, help your friend find a grown-up they can count on." What can you say to invite a friend to talk? Next time, we'll walk toward Heart Hill with a map, but Haru and Riko won't agree about the way. | A+C (block rule) | Ms. Emma reads her own script, so her own narration "Ms. Emma says / asks / smiles / sits ... with Ms. Emma" -> "I say / I ask / I smile / I sit ... | 1121 | `teacherScript` | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, Tell me what happened. Today, we set off for the spring at the top of Heart Hill with a map from Grandma Rowan. Above the hill, the Great Rainbow shows its red and orange stripes. Soon we reach a fork in the path. Haru grabs the map. We go left. It's shorter! Riko frowns. But Grandma Rowan said the right path is better. Haru insists, Left! I've got the map! Riko raises her voice. You never listen! Haru raises his voice, too. Then thick gray fog rolls in around us. Puff turns gray and whispers, Oh dear. Haru and Riko had a fight about the map. Say it with me. Haru and Riko had a fight about the map. Good. Ms. Emma calmly says, Everyone, stop here and stay close to me. We'll wait until the fog thins. We're all together, and nobody is lost. Let's read a page from Puff's Feelings Book. "Puff's Feelings Book, Page Twelve: When Friends Argue. Even the best of friends argue sometimes. That's normal, and it doesn't mean the friendship is over. An argument often starts with something small, like a map, a game or the last cookie. Then the feelings get bigger. One friend feels ignored. The other friend feels that nobody trusts them. Voices get louder, and nobody is really listening anymore. In the Highlands, this is when the fog rolls in. The fog here is not dangerous, but it makes everything hard to see, just like anger does. When the fog comes, stop walking. Stand still, take a slow breath and wait. Little by little, the fog will thin, and you will see your friend again. Remember, fog never lasts forever." What often starts an argument? Something small, like a map, a game or the last cookie. What should you do when the fog comes? Stop walking, stand still and take a slow breath. So we wait. Haru and Riko stand apart without a word. Now, phonics. The same words can sound kind or unkind, depending on your voice. When a friend has sad news, use a low, soft voice that falls. Oh no. When a friend has good news, use a high, bright voice that goes up and down. That's great! When you're surprised or checking, let your voice go up. Really? Next time, someone will find the words to say sorry. | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, Tell me what happened. Haru raises his voice, too. Then thick gray fog rolls in around us. Puff turns gray and whispers, Oh dear. Haru and Riko had a fight about the map. Say it with me. The same words can sound kind or unkind, depending on your voice. When a friend has sad news, use a low, soft voice that falls. Oh no. Next time, someone will find the words to say sorry. | A+C (block rule) | Ms. Emma reads her own script, so her own narration "Ms. Emma says / asks / smiles / sits ... with Ms. Emma" -> "I say / I ask / I smile / I sit ... Next time, someone will find the words to say sorry. | A+C, A+B | the quote has no speaker, so the voice reads it as my own narration; add the speaker (house style 'X says, ...'); 'We go left' is not what a child insisting would say; safety: telling children fog is 'not dangerous' can carry over to real hill fog; the page already teaches stop, stand still and wait |

| 1123 | `teacherScript` | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, I'm sorry. I didn't mean to hurt you. Today, the fog is thinner, but it hasn't gone away. We can see a stone wall and a few sheep again, although Heart Hill is still hidden. Ms. Emma says, Saying sorry was a great first step. Now let's think back. What happened, and what can we do differently next time? Puff pulls a small card out of the mist. It's a Think-Back Card. Haru reads the first question and thinks hard. Then he turns to Riko and says, I should have listened to you. Say it with me. I should have listened to you. Excellent! Riko smiles a little. And I could have explained it more calmly, she says. Let's read the Think-Back Card that Haru and Riko filled in together. "Puff's Think-Back Card. Question one: What happened? Haru: I grabbed the map and said we had to go left. Riko: I wanted to go right, and I shouted. Question two: How did you feel? Haru: At first, I felt proud of my plan. Then I felt bad. Riko: I felt ignored and frustrated. Question three: What should you have done? Haru: I should have listened to Riko. I should have asked everyone what they thought. Riko: I could have told Haru my reason before I got upset. Question four: What will you do next time? Haru: I'll share the map. Riko: I'll take a deep breath before I speak. Puff's note: Thinking back is not about feeling bad. It's about learning, so that the next walk is a happier one." How did Riko feel? Ignored and frustrated. What will Haru do next time? Share the map. As they finish the card, a soft breeze blows, and the fog drifts away a little more. Puff changes from gray to a light silver. Now, phonics. Let's practice our kind voices again. When something sad happens, say it softly and let your voice fall. I'm so sorry. When a friend does something good, sound bright and go up, then down. Good for you! When you want to check on someone, go up at the end. Is everything all right? Next time, will the fog finally clear? | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, I'm sorry. I didn't mean to hurt you. | 1124 | `teacherScript` | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, I should have listened to you. Today, Haru and Riko stand up from the rock. Haru holds out the map. Let's read it together this time, he says. Then he holds out his other hand. Riko, let's make up. Friends? Say it with me. Let's make up. Friends? Great! Riko grins and shakes his hand. Friends! At that moment, something wonderful happens. The fog begins to lift, like a curtain opening on a stage. First we see the stone walls, then the purple heather, and then the top of Heart Hill, shining in the sun. Warm sunbeams fall on our faces, and Puff turns bright gold. Together, we take the right path, and soon we reach the spring. The water is clear and cold, and it sparkles like glass. Beside it, there is a little wooden box with a letter inside. Let's read it. "Dear friends, If you are reading this, you have reached the Spring of Heart Hill. Well done! Many people who come here have had a hard walk. Some got tired. Some got lost. Some had an argument on the way. When I was young, I argued with my best friend on this very path, and we didn't speak for a whole morning. Then we sat down here, drank this cold water and started to laugh. Making up is like this spring. It's simple, but it makes you feel fresh again. You don't need perfect words. Say sorry, say what you will do next time, and then do something together. Drink some water, look at the view and enjoy your friendship. With love, Grandma Rowan." What happened when Grandma Rowan argued with her best friend? They didn't speak for a whole morning. What does she say making up is like? This spring. Haru and Riko scoop up the water and drink it at the same time. Then Birimo slips on a wet stone and sits down with a splash! Did it hurt? Not really, he says, but now his bottom is wet. Everyone laughs. Now, phonics. Let's listen to three more kind voices. Kiko's sandwich fell in the stream. That's too bad. Low and falling. The sun came out! How wonderful! Bright, up and down. Birimo slipped on the stones. Did it hurt? Up at the end. Next time, we'll share what we learned with the children of Heatherbrook. | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, I should have listened to you. Today, Haru and Riko stand up from the rock. Haru holds out the map. Let's read it together this time, he says. Then he holds out his other hand. Riko, let's make up. Friends? Say it with me. Let's make up. Friends? Great! Riko grins and shakes his hand. Friends! At that moment, something wonderful happens. Warm sunbeams fall on our faces, and Puff turns bright gold. Together, we take the right path, and soon we reach the spring. The water is clear and cold, and it sparkles like glass. Beside it, there is a little wooden box with a letter inside. Let's read it. "Dear friends, If you are reading this, you have reached the Spring of Heart Hill. Well done! Many people who come here have had a hard walk. Some got tired. Some got lost. Some had an argument on the way. When I was young, I argued with my best friend on this very path, and we didn't speak for a whole morning. Then we sat down here, drank this cold water and started to laugh. Making up is like this spring. It's simple, but it makes you feel fresh again. You don't need perfect words. Say sorry, say what you will do next time, and then do something together. Drink some water, look at the view and enjoy your friendship. With love, Grandma Rowan." What happened when Grandma Rowan argued with her best friend? They didn't speak for a whole morning. What does she say making up is like? This spring. Haru and Riko scoop up the water and drink it at the same time. Then Birimo slips on a wet stone and sits down with a splash! I ask, Did it hurt? Just a little, he says, but now his bottom is wet. Everyone laughs. Now, phonics. Let's listen to three more kind voices. Kiko's sandwich fell in the stream. That's too bad. Low and falling. The sun came out! How wonderful! Bright, up and down. Birimo slipped on the stones. Did it hurt? Up at the end. Next time, we'll share what we learned with the children of Heatherbrook. | objective | story: the conversation and seg7 have me ask and Birimo answer 'Just a little' (the target answer), not 'Not really'; the question also had no speaker |
