| 1125 | `teacherScript` | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, Let's make up. Friends? Today, we are back in Heatherbrook, and Grandma Rowan has a request. The children at the little village school sometimes argue, too, she says. Could you make a poster to help them? So, at Grandma Rowan's big kitchen table, we get to work. Haru draws the pictures, Riko writes the words, Kiko adds the colors, and Birimo adds lots of tiny hearts. Then we carry the poster to the village school, where twelve children are waiting. Haru and Riko stand in front of them together. Riko points to the poster and reads the four steps. Calm down. Listen. Say how you feel. Find a solution. Say it with me. Calm down. Listen. Say how you feel. Find a solution. Fantastic! Let's read the poster. "Four Steps to Solve a Fight. Step one: Calm down. When you feel angry, stop. Take three deep breaths, or count to ten. Big feelings are OK, but it's hard to think clearly when you're upset. Step two: Listen. Let the other person speak, and don't interrupt. Try to understand their reason. Step three: Say how you feel. Use I sentences, like I felt sad when you took the map. Never call people names. Step four: Find a solution. Think of an idea that works for both of you. You could take turns, share, or choose together. Then shake hands and make up. Remember, everyone argues sometimes. What matters is what you do next. Made by Haru, Riko, Kiko and Birimo, with help from Puff." What can you do in step one? Take three deep breaths, or count to ten. What should you do in step two? Let the other person speak, and don't interrupt. The children clap, and a small boy puts up his hand. I had a fight with my sister yesterday, he says. That must be hard, says Kiko gently. But we made up this morning, he adds. That's great! At that moment, warm sunshine pours through the window. Look! The yellow stripe came back to the Great Rainbow! Now it has red, orange and yellow. Now, phonics. Let's review. That must be hard, low and soft. That's great! Bright and happy. Make up, link the words and say up strongly. Lonely is lone plus l-y, so keep the e. Next time, Riko has a plan that starts very early in the morning. | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, Let's make up. Friends? Today, we are back in Heatherbrook, and Grandma Rowan has a request. | 1126 | `teacherScript` | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, Calm down. Listen. Say how you feel. Find a solution. Today, we are having dinner at Grandma Rowan's farmhouse. The rainbow above Heart Hill now has three colors, and Puff says the next color needs a new heart skill. Grandma Rowan tells us that the sunrise from the top of Heart Hill is the most beautiful sight in the highlands. Riko's eyes light up. But Haru yawns. I want to sleep late tomorrow. And I want pancakes for breakfast, says Birimo. Riko thinks for a moment. Then she says, Wouldn't it be better if we left early? We could see the sunrise, the air is cool in the morning, and we can take the pancakes with us for a picnic on the hilltop! Say it with me. Wouldn't it be better if we left early? Very good! Riko gives reasons, and she thinks about what everyone wants. Let's read a page from Puff's Feelings Book. "Puff's Feelings Book, Page Twenty: How to Persuade Kindly. Sometimes you want your friends to agree with your idea. That's fine! But persuading is not the same as bossing people around. Here are some tips. First, think about what the other person wants. Second, give clear reasons, and explain how your idea helps everyone, not only you. Third, use gentle questions, like Wouldn't it be better if we tried this? or How about this? Fourth, listen to their worries and answer them. Maybe your friend is tired, or hungry, or a little scared. Finally, remember that your friend can say no. A good persuader smiles, respects the answer and stays a good friend. If they say yes, wonderful! If they say no, that's OK too." What is the first tip? Think about what the other person wants. What should you remember at the end? Your friend can say no. Haru thinks about it. Cool air, a sunrise and pancakes on the hilltop? OK, you've persuaded me! Birimo is over the moon. Ms. Emma sets her alarm clock for half past four. Now, phonics. Today, we learn idioms about feelings. When you are very, very happy, you're over the moon. When you feel sad, you feel blue. When something is very easy, it's a piece of cake. But getting up at half past four is not a piece of cake! Next time, at dawn, Birimo has an idea of his own. | Hello, everyone! | 1127 | `teacherScript` | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, Wouldn't it be better if we left early? Today, it's half past four in the morning. The stars are still out. We walk quietly out of the farmhouse with Ms. Emma and our flashlights. Puff glows softly beside us like a little lamp. At the foot of Heart Hill, there are two paths. The short path goes straight up. The long path curves along a high ridge. Birimo stops. Let's take the long path! Haru frowns. But it's longer, and we might miss the sunrise. Birimo says, Grandma Rowan told me a secret. From the ridge, you can see a sea of clouds at dawn. Trust me. It'll be worth it. Say it with me. Trust me. It'll be worth it. Wonderful! Ms. Emma checks her watch. We have time. Let's read the note Grandma Rowan wrote on the back of our map. "The Ridge Path. This path is about twenty minutes longer than the short path, but it is my favorite walk in the whole highlands. It is wide and safe, with a stone wall on one side, so just walk steadily and stay together. On cool, still mornings, the valleys below fill with white mist. When the sun comes up, the mist turns pink and gold, and it looks like a soft, shining sea. The hills poke out of it like little islands. I call it the sea of clouds. Not many people see it, because most people take the shortcut or stay in bed! If you reach the big flat rock before sunrise, sit down, eat something warm and wait. You won't be sorry. Grandma Rowan." How much longer is the ridge path? About twenty minutes. Why don't many people see the sea of clouds? Because most people take the shortcut or stay in bed. We reach the big flat rock just in time. Below us, the valleys are full of white clouds. Then the sun rises, and the whole sea turns pink and gold. Haru whispers, Birimo, you were right. It was worth it. Now, phonics. More idioms! When you are nervous, you have butterflies in your stomach. When you are super happy, you're on cloud nine. When you say the first friendly words to someone new, you break the ice. Next time, back in the village, we'll meet someone who needs a friend. | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, Wouldn't it be better if we left early? Today, it's half past four in the morning. | 1128 | `teacherScript` | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, Trust me. It'll be worth it. Today, we're back in Heatherbrook. On the village green, children are playing tag. But at the edge of the green, a boy named Finn is sitting on his own. He watches the game, but he doesn't join in, and a tiny gray cloud floats above his head. Kiko says, I think he wants to play, but he's too shy to ask. Riko has an idea. At my school, we have a buddy bench. When you sit on it, it means you'd like someone to play with, and other kids come and invite you. We go to see Mr. Bram, the village leader. He has a big gray beard. He listens, strokes his beard and says, Hmm. A bench takes money and work. Haru answers, If we do this, everyone will benefit. Say it with me. If we do this, everyone will benefit. Super! Let's read the idea sheet we wrote for Mr. Bram. "Our Idea: A Buddy Bench for Heatherbrook. What is it? A buddy bench is a bench where you sit when you want someone to play with. When other children see someone on the bench, they go over and say, Do you want to play with us? Why do we need it? Some children feel shy, and it's hard for them to ask to join a game. New children and visitors often don't know anyone. A buddy bench makes it easy to find a friend. Who will benefit? Shy children, new children, visitors and everyone who wants more friends. How can we do it? There is old wood in the village barn. We will help paint the bench, and we will show the children how it works. Thank you for reading!" Who will benefit? Shy children, new children, visitors and everyone who wants more friends. Where is the wood? In the village barn. Mr. Bram reads it and smiles. It's a fine idea, but the village decides together. Come to the village hall in two days and tell everyone. Haru gulps. A speech? Riko says, Keep your chin up. We'll do it together. Now, phonics. If you feel a little sick, you're under the weather. To tell someone to stay brave, say keep your chin up. If you suddenly feel too nervous to do something, you get cold feet. Next time, we'll get some surprising invitations. | Hello, everyone! But at the edge of the green, a boy named Finn is sitting on his own. He watches the game, but he doesn't join in, and a tiny gray cloud floats above his head. Kiko says, I think he wants to play, but he's too shy to ask. Riko has an idea. She says, At my school, we have a buddy bench. When you sit on it, it means you'd like someone to play with, and other kids come and invite you. We go to see Mr. Bram, the village leader. He has a big gray beard. He listens, strokes his beard and says, Hmm. A bench takes money and work. Haru answers, If we do this, everyone will benefit. Say it with me. If we do this, everyone will benefit. Super! Let's read the idea sheet we wrote for Mr. Bram. "Our Idea: A Buddy Bench for Heatherbrook. What is it? A buddy bench is a bench where you sit when you want someone to play with. When other children see someone on the bench, they go over and say, Do you want to play with us? Why do we need it? Some children feel shy, and it's hard for them to ask to join a game. New children and visitors often don't know anyone. A buddy bench makes it easy to find a friend. Who will benefit? Shy children, new children, visitors and everyone who wants more friends. How can we do it? There is old wood in the village barn. We will help paint the bench, and we will show the children how it works. Thank you for reading!" Who will benefit? Shy children, new children, visitors and everyone who wants more friends. Where is the wood? In the village barn. Mr. Bram reads it, smiles and says, It's a fine idea, but the village decides together. Come to the village hall in two days and tell everyone. Haru gulps. A speech? Riko says, Keep your chin up. We'll do it together. Now, phonics. If you feel a little sick, you're under the weather. To tell someone to stay brave, say keep your chin up. If you suddenly feel too nervous to do something, you get cold feet. Next time, we'll get some surprising invitations. | A+C | without a speaker 'At my school' reads as my own school; add 'She says,'; the quote has no speaker, so the voice reads it as my own narration; add the speaker (house style 'X says, ...') |

| 1130 | `teacherScript` | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, Thanks for asking, but I'd rather not. Today is the Persuasion Challenge at the village hall. The hall is full of villagers sitting on long wooden benches. Grandma Rowan is in the front row with Biscuit at her feet, and Finn is sitting next to his brother. Mr. Bram stands at the front. Haru's hands are shaking. Kiko asks, Are you OK? Haru nods. I'm nervous, but I'm ready. One by one, we speak. Kiko talks about new children. Haru talks about visitors. Birimo makes everyone laugh with a funny drawing. Then Riko steps forward and begins, Let me tell you why this is a great idea. Say it with me. Let me tell you why this is a great idea. Brilliant! Let's read Riko's speech. "Good evening, everyone. Let me tell you why this is a great idea. Imagine you are new in a village. You see children playing, and you want to join them, but you feel too shy to ask. Maybe you feel frustrated, or a little lonely. A buddy bench can help. When you sit on it, everyone knows that you want someone to play with. Then a kind child comes over and says, Come and play with us! First, it helps shy children and new children. Second, it teaches all of us to notice each other. Third, it's easy. There is wood in the barn, and we will help paint it. Heatherbrook is a warm village. With a buddy bench, it can be even warmer. Thank you for listening." What are Riko's three reasons? It helps shy and new children, it teaches us to notice each other, and it's easy. Where is the wood? In the barn. Then Mr. Bram asks the villagers to vote. Hands go up everywhere. Mr. Bram smiles under his big gray beard. The answer is yes! The village carpenter offers to build the bench next week. Finn's little gray cloud melts away, and he smiles. At that moment, sunlight fills the windows. Look! The green stripe came back to the Great Rainbow! Now there are four colors: red, orange, yellow and green. Now, phonics. Let's review. Over the moon means very happy. Are you OK? Let your voice go up. Cheer up, link the words and say up strongly. Frustrated has no extra e. Next time, we'll talk about people we look up to. | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, Thanks for asking, but I'd rather not. | 1132 | `teacherScript` | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, I look up to my aunt because she's so patient. This morning, Mr. Bram knocks on the farmhouse door with exciting news. On Saturday, the village hall will hold an Interview Show, and he wants us to be the interviewers! Our guest will be a very special person, but he won't tell us who yet. He just winks and strokes his big gray beard. Ms. Emma says, A good interview is like a good conversation. Don't just ask yes-or-no questions. Ask questions that open doors. She writes three questions on the board. Why? How did you feel at that moment? What did you learn? Haru practices by interviewing Kiko about the welcome party, when she played her flute again. He asks, What happened? Kiko says, My hands were shaking before I started. Then Haru asks the deep question. How did you feel at that moment? Say it with me. How did you feel at that moment? Excellent! Let's read Puff's interview tips card. "Puff's Five Interview Tips. One: Start with an easy question to help your guest relax. Two: Ask open questions that start with why, how or what. A yes-or-no question gives you one word, but an open question gives you a story. Three: Ask about feelings. How did you feel at that moment? What was it like? Four: Listen carefully, and ask a follow-up question. If your guest says, I was nervous, you can ask, What helped you feel better? Five: Near the end, ask, What did you learn? Remember, an interview is not a test. Be thoughtful and kind. If your guest doesn't want to answer a question, that's OK. Just say, No problem, and move on. And always say thank you at the end!" What kind of question gives you a story? An open question. What can you say if your guest doesn't want to answer? No problem. Kiko answers Haru with a big smile. At first I was scared, but then I felt really proud. Puff glows gold. Now, phonics. Thoughtful ends in -ful. It means full of thought, so a thoughtful person thinks about others. Sensitive ends in -ive. A sensitive person notices other people's feelings. Courageous ends in -ous. Keep the e in courage, then add -ous! It means very brave. Next time, we'll find out who our special guest is. | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, I look up to my aunt because she's so patient. | 1133 | `teacherScript` | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, How did you feel at that moment? Today, Mr. Bram finally tells us the name of our guest for the Interview Show. It's Grandma Rowan! She laughs and says, Oh my, I'm not famous! But Mr. Bram says that everyone in Heatherbrook has a story about her. So this evening, we sit by the fire in her farmhouse and do a practice interview. Biscuit rests his head on her knee. Kiko holds the notebook, Haru holds a pretend microphone, and Ms. Emma sits with us, ready to help. Riko asks the first question. Grandma Rowan, what was your childhood like? Say it with me. What was your childhood like? Wonderful! Let's read Kiko's interview notes. "Interview Notes: Grandma Rowan. Childhood: She grew up on a farm in these highlands, with sheep, chickens and an old pony. She was a cheerful, adventurous girl who loved climbing stone walls. Dream: She wanted to be an animal doctor, because she loved caring for sick lambs. Problem: The nearest school was far away, over three hills. She walked there every day, in sunshine, wind and rain. Hard moment: She failed an important exam once. She felt terrible and cried behind the barn. What helped: Her teacher was very supportive. She said, One exam doesn't decide who you are. Ending: She studied hard, tried again and passed. She became the animal doctor for the whole highlands! Now she is retired and keeps sheep." How far away was the school? Over three hills. What did her teacher say? One exam doesn't decide who you are. Then Haru asks a follow-up question. How did you feel when you failed the exam? Grandma Rowan looks into the fire for a moment. Very disappointed, she says. But I learned that falling down is part of climbing. Puff turns a soft gray, and then a warm gold again. Now, phonics. Cheerful ends in -ful. It means full of cheer, so a cheerful person smiles a lot. Supportive ends in -ive. A supportive person helps you keep going. Adventurous ends in -ous. An adventurous person loves exploring and trying new things. Young Rowan was cheerful and adventurous, and her teacher was supportive. Next time, we'll turn these notes into an interview article. | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, How did you feel at that moment? Today, Mr. Bram finally tells us the name of our guest for the Interview Show. It's Grandma Rowan! She laughs and says, Oh my, I'm not famous! But Mr. Bram says that everyone in Heatherbrook has a story about her. So this evening, we sit by the fire in her farmhouse and do a practice interview. Biscuit rests his head on her knee. Kiko holds the notebook, Haru holds a pretend microphone, and I sit with them, ready to help. Riko asks the first question. Grandma Rowan, what was your childhood like? Say it with me. What was your childhood like? Wonderful! Let's read Kiko's interview notes. "Interview Notes: Grandma Rowan. Childhood: She grew up on a farm in these highlands, with sheep, chickens and an old pony. She was a cheerful, adventurous girl who loved climbing stone walls. Dream: She wanted to be an animal doctor, because she loved caring for sick lambs. Problem: The nearest school was far away, over three hills. She walked there every day, in sunshine, wind and rain. Hard moment: She failed an important exam once. She felt terrible and cried behind the barn. What helped: Her teacher was very supportive. She said, One exam doesn't decide who you are. Ending: She studied hard, tried again and passed. She became the animal doctor for the whole highlands! Now she is retired and keeps sheep." How far away was the school? Over three hills. What did her teacher say? One exam doesn't decide who you are. Then Haru asks a follow-up question. How did you feel when you failed the exam? Grandma Rowan looks into the fire for a moment. Very disappointed, she says. But I learned that falling down is part of climbing. Puff turns a soft gray, and then a warm gold again. Now, phonics. Cheerful ends in -ful. It means full of cheer, so a cheerful person smiles a lot. Supportive ends in -ive. A supportive person helps you keep going. Adventurous ends in -ous. An adventurous person loves exploring and trying new things. Young Rowan was cheerful and adventurous, and her teacher was supportive. Next time, we'll turn these notes into an interview article. | A+C (block rule) | Ms. Emma reads her own script, so her own narration "Ms. Emma says / asks / smiles / sits ... with Ms. Emma" -> "I say / I ask / I smile / I sit ... | 1134 | `teacherScript` | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, What was your childhood like? Today, we're turning our interview into an article for the Heatherbrook Village News, a little newspaper that is pinned up outside the village shop every Friday. Ms. Emma explains how an article works. First, you need a headline that catches the reader's eye. Next, an opening that tells who, what and where. Then you share the most interesting parts of the story, with quotes. A quote is the exact words that someone said. In writing, we put those words inside quotation marks and say who said them. Riko reads her favorite quote from our notes. Grandma Rowan said, I never gave up. Ms. Emma writes it on the board with quotation marks. 'I never gave up,' she told me. Say it with me. 'I never gave up,' she told me. Perfect! Let's read the article we wrote together. "From Lambs to Legends: Meet Grandma Rowan. Everyone in Heatherbrook knows Grandma Rowan, the kind shepherd in the woolly hat. But did you know she was once the animal doctor for the whole highlands? As a girl, she dreamed of caring for animals. Her school was three hills away, and she walked there every day. 'The wind tried to push me home, but I kept walking,' she laughed. When she failed an important exam, she wanted to quit. Her supportive teacher helped her try again. 'I never gave up,' she told me. Today she is retired, but she still looks after her sheep, with help from her playful sheepdog, Biscuit. Her advice for children? 'Be attentive to animals and people. They will tell you what they need.'" Where is the Village News pinned up? Outside the village shop. What is Grandma Rowan's advice? Be attentive to animals and people. Birimo wants to add a joke at the bottom of the page. He writes, Biscuit would like everyone to know that he is the real boss of the farm. We all laugh, and Ms. Emma says we can keep it. Now, phonics. Playful ends in -ful. It means full of play, like Biscuit chasing his tail. Attentive ends in -ive. An attentive person pays close attention. Humorous ends in -ous. A humorous person makes people laugh, just like Birimo! Next time, it's the big Interview Show at the village hall! | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, What was your childhood like? Today, we're turning our interview into an article for the Heatherbrook Village News, a little newspaper that is pinned up outside the village shop every Friday. I explain how an article works. First, you need a headline that catches the reader's eye. Next, an opening that tells who, what and where. Then you share the most interesting parts of the story, with quotes. A quote is the exact words that someone said. In writing, we put those words inside quotation marks and say who said them. Riko reads her favorite quote from our notes. Grandma Rowan said, I never gave up. I write it on the board with quotation marks. 'I never gave up,' she told me. Say it with me. 'I never gave up,' she told me. Perfect! Let's read the article we wrote together. "From Lambs to Legends: Meet Grandma Rowan. Everyone in Heatherbrook knows Grandma Rowan, the kind shepherd in the woolly hat. But did you know she was once the animal doctor for the whole highlands? As a girl, she dreamed of caring for animals. Her school was three hills away, and she walked there every day. 'The wind tried to push me home, but I kept walking,' she laughed. When she failed an important exam, she wanted to quit. Her supportive teacher helped her try again. 'I never gave up,' she told me. Today she is retired, but she still looks after her sheep, with help from her playful sheepdog, Biscuit. Her advice for children? 'Be attentive to animals and people. They will tell you what they need.'" Where is the Village News pinned up? Outside the village shop. What is Grandma Rowan's advice? Be attentive to animals and people. Birimo wants to add a joke at the bottom of the page. He writes, Biscuit would like everyone to know that he is the real boss of the farm. We all laugh, and I say we can keep it. Now, phonics. Playful ends in -ful. It means full of play, like Biscuit chasing his tail. Attentive ends in -ive. An attentive person pays close attention. Humorous ends in -ous. A humorous person makes people laugh, just like Birimo! Next time, it's the big Interview Show at the village hall! | A+C (block rule) | Ms. Emma reads her own script, so her own narration "Ms. Emma says / asks / smiles / sits ... with Ms. Emma" -> "I say / I ask / I smile / I sit ... (with me / together)" (same as W17-36, say + subtitles + teacherScript); JA エマ先生 -> わたし |

| 1136 | `teacherScript` | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, Our guest today is Grandma Rowan. Today, a gentle rain is falling on Heatherbrook, so Grandma Rowan has a plan. She brings out a small brass bell and says, Welcome to the Heart Idiom Quiz! An idiom is a group of words with a special meaning. You can't guess it from each single word. For example, a piece of cake（とても かんたん）doesn't really mean cake. It means very easy. Birimo looks worried. No cake? Grandma Rowan laughs and puts a plate of real cake on the table, too. Haru rings the bell first and says, Over the moon means very, very happy! Correct! Kiko knows feel blue. It means feel sad. Riko knows butterflies in my stomach. It means feeling nervous. Kiko says she felt a little jealous when Haru rang the bell so fast, but then she rings it first for the next question! Then Birimo winks at Ms. Emma and asks, Learning English is a piece of cake now? Ms. Emma laughs. Not yet! But we're getting better every day. Say it with me. Learning English is a piece of cake now? Not yet! Ha! Great! Let's read the quiz card. "Heart Idiom Quiz. Question one: Mia's team won the final match. She is over the moon. How does Mia feel? Answer: very happy. Question two: Tom's best friend moved to another town, and Tom feels blue. How does Tom feel? Answer: sad. Question three: Before her dance show, Lily has butterflies in her stomach. How does Lily feel? Answer: nervous. Question four: The spelling test was a piece of cake for Ben. Was it hard for him? Answer: no, it was very easy. Bonus question: Which idiom means you suddenly feel too nervous to do something? Answer: get cold feet. Well done! Idioms make your English colorful, so try to use them at the right moment." How does Tom feel? Sad. What does a piece of cake mean? Very easy. Now, phonics. Let's review. In jealous, the e and a together sound like a short e. In look up to, link look and up, and say UP strongly. Oh no goes down, soft and slow. Butterflies in my stomach means nervous. Creative ends in -ive. Riko makes up a creative new saying. As warm as Grandma Rowan's kitchen! Next time, we'll write letters to people who are important to us. | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, Our guest today is Grandma Rowan. | 1137 | `teacherScript` | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, Learning English is a piece of cake now? Not yet! Today, Grandma Rowan gives each of us a sheet of creamy writing paper and an envelope with a tiny sprig of heather on it. In the highlands, she says, we write the most important things in letters, because a letter can be read again and again. Haru writes to his swimming coach. Riko writes to her aunt. Birimo writes to Puff, even though Puff is floating right next to him! Kiko is quiet. She wants to write to her best friend, who moved to another town last spring. A small gray cloud appears over Kiko's head. Puff floats close and asks, How are you really feeling? Kiko says, I miss her. But I want her to know she's still my friend. Then she writes, I wanted to tell you how much you mean to me. Say it with me. I wanted to tell you how much you mean to me. Beautiful! Let's read Kiko's letter. "Dear Sora, How are you? I'm writing from the Highlands of the Heart, where the weather changes with your feelings. It's sunny right now, because I'm thinking about you. I was disappointed when you moved away, and at first I didn't want to talk about it. But here, I learned to open up. So, I wanted to tell you how much you mean to me. You were always so supportive. When I was nervous before my recital, you held my hand. You have a heart of gold. I hope your new town is fun and you're making lots of friends. Please write back soon and tell me everything! I'm sending you a pressed heather flower. Your friend forever, Kiko" Where is Kiko writing from? The Highlands of the Heart. What did Sora do before Kiko's recital? She held Kiko's hand. When Kiko finishes, the gray cloud melts into sunshine. Haru says, Good for you, Kiko. That was brave. Tomorrow, we'll post our letters with Ms. Emma. Now, phonics. Let's review. Disappointed has one s and double p. In open up, link open and up, and say UP strongly. Good for you goes high and then down, bright and happy. A heart of gold means very kind. Supportive ends in -ive. Next time, we'll start a kindness chain in Heatherbrook! | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, Learning English is a piece of cake now? Not yet! Today, Grandma Rowan gives each of us a sheet of creamy writing paper and an envelope with a tiny sprig of heather on it. I was disappointed when you moved away, and at first I didn't want to talk about it. But here, I learned to open up. So, I wanted to tell you how much you mean to me. You were always so supportive. When I was nervous before my recital, you held my hand. You have a heart of gold. I hope your new town is fun and you're making lots of friends. Please write back soon and tell me everything! When Kiko finishes, the gray cloud melts into sunshine. Haru says, Good for you, Kiko. That was brave. Tomorrow, we'll post our letters together. Now, phonics. Let's review. Supportive ends in -ive. Next time, we'll start a kindness chain in Heatherbrook! | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, Learning English is a piece of cake now? Not yet! Today, Grandma Rowan gives each of us a sheet of creamy writing paper and an envelope with a tiny sprig of heather on it. In the highlands, she says, we write the most important things in letters, because a letter can be read again and again. Haru writes to his swimming coach. Riko writes to her aunt. Birimo writes to Puff, even though Puff is floating right next to him! Kiko is quiet. She wants to write to her best friend, who moved to another town last spring. A small gray cloud appears over Kiko's head. Puff floats close and asks, How are you really feeling? Kiko says, I miss her. But I want her to know she's still my friend. Then she writes, I wanted to tell you how much you mean to me. Say it with me. I wanted to tell you how much you mean to me. Beautiful! Let's read Kiko's letter. "Dear Sora, How are you? I'm writing from the Highlands of the Heart, where the weather changes with your feelings. It's sunny right now, because I'm thinking about you. I was disappointed when you moved away, and at first I didn't want to talk about it. But here, I learned to open up. So, I wanted to tell you how much you mean to me. You were always so supportive. When I was nervous before my recital, you held my hand. You have a heart of gold. I hope your new town is fun and you're making lots of friends. Please write back soon and tell me everything! I'm sending you a pressed heather flower. Your friend forever, Kiko" Where is Kiko writing from? The Highlands of the Heart. What did Sora do before Kiko's recital? She held Kiko's hand. When Kiko finishes, the gray cloud melts into sunshine. Haru says, Good for you, Kiko. That was brave. Tomorrow, we'll mail our letters together. Now, phonics. Let's review. Disappointed has one s and double p. In open up, link open and up, and say UP strongly. Good for you goes high and then down, bright and happy. A heart of gold means very kind. Supportive ends in -ive. Next time, we'll start a kindness chain in Heatherbrook! | objective (US policy) | vocab post -> mail (US) |

| 1138 | `teacherScript` | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, I wanted to tell you how much you mean to me. This morning, the buddy bench is finally ready! Mr. Bram painted it bright red, and it stands under the old oak tree on the village green. Ms. Emma has an idea. Let's start a kindness chain. Each person says one kind word or does one kind thing for someone, and then that person passes it on. Haru starts. He tells the baker, Your bread smells amazing! The baker smiles and gives a warm roll to a tired farmer. The farmer thanks the postman for bringing letters in the rain. The postman helps a girl named Ellie carry a heavy basket. Then Ellie sees Finn sitting on the buddy bench. She sits down next to him and asks, Do you want to play? Finn looks up. Really? You want to play with me? Soon they are laughing and running together. Ms. Emma says, One kind word can change someone's day. Say it with me. One kind word can change someone's day. Wonderful! Let's read the poster we made for the village shop. "Join the Heatherbrook Kindness Chain! How does it work? It's easy. Say one kind word or do one kind thing for someone. Then ask them to pass it on. Here are some ideas. Say thank you to someone who helps you. Tell a friend who feels down, Keep your chin up. Stand up for someone who is being left out. Smile at someone new. Help carry a heavy bag. Sit with someone on the buddy bench. Kindness doesn't have to be big. Even a cheerful hello can change someone's day. When you are kind, you feel happy too, and the sky over the highlands gets sunnier! How long can our chain be? Let's find out together!" What can you say to a friend who feels down? Keep your chin up. Where can you sit with someone? On the buddy bench. By sunset, the chain has forty-two kind words! Now, phonics. Let's review. Exhausted has a silent h after the x. In stand up for, link stand and up, and say UP strongly. Really with a rising voice shows you're surprised. Keep your chin up means stay brave. Cheerful ends in -ful. By the evening, we're exhausted but cheerful! Next time, we'll take a goodbye walk and look back on our adventure. | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, I wanted to tell you how much you mean to me. | 1139 | `teacherScript` | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, One kind word can change someone's day. Today, we take a goodbye walk around the highlands with Ms. Emma, Puff, Grandma Rowan and Biscuit. We pass the muddy spot where Riko slipped, and she laughs. We pass the village hall where we gave our speeches, and the fork in the path where Haru and Riko had their fight. I'm glad we made up, says Haru. Me too, says Riko. But Birimo is very quiet, and a tiny rain cloud is following him. Puff floats over and asks, How are you really feeling? Birimo says, I'm going to miss Biscuit so much. I've felt sad all morning. Haru puts his arm around him and says, That must be hard for you. I should have noticed sooner. Say it with me. That must be hard for you. I should have noticed sooner. Well done! Let's read the last page of Puff's Feelings Book. "What We Learned in the Highlands of the Heart. First, we learned many feeling words. Now we can say that we are over the moon, a little annoyed, or embarrassed but relieved. Next, we learned to listen. We say, That must be hard, or Tell me what happened, and we don't interrupt. Then Haru and Riko had a fight. They learned to say, I didn't mean to hurt you, and I should have listened, and they made up. After that, we learned to persuade with kind reasons, and to say no politely. Finally, we interviewed Grandma Rowan and learned that asking good questions is a way of caring. Most of all, we learned that every feeling is OK, and sharing it helps." What do good listeners say? That must be hard, or Tell me what happened. What did we learn most of all? Every feeling is OK, and sharing it helps. Just then, Biscuit runs up and licks Birimo's nose. Birimo giggles. Grandma Rowan promises to send him a drawing of Biscuit every month. Birimo's rain cloud disappears, and for the first time, the sky over the highlands is completely clear. Now, phonics. Let's review. Confused has one f. Work out means end well, and everything worked out in the end. I'm so sorry is said low and soft. Under the weather means a little sick. Courageous keeps the e in courage. Next time, it's the Heart Festival on Heart Hill! | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, One kind word can change someone's day. | 1140 | `teacherScript` | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, That must be hard for you. I should have noticed sooner. Today is the Heart Festival on Heart Hill! The whole village climbs the hill together. Grandma Rowan walks with Biscuit, Mr. Bram carries a basket of scones, and Finn holds hands with his new friend Ellie. Under the Great Rainbow, villagers take turns sharing their feelings. A boy says he was annoyed when his kite got stuck in a tree, but he turned to his big sister, and she helped him get it down. A farmer says he is on cloud nine, because a new lamb was born this morning. Everyone shouts, Congratulations! Then Ms. Emma asks us to share what we learned. Kiko steps forward and says, Words can heal hearts. Say it with me. Words can heal hearts. Wonderful! Let's read Kiko's speech. "When we came to the highlands, the Great Rainbow had no colors, because people had stopped sharing their feelings. Here, we learned that it's OK to feel sad, nervous or annoyed. We learned to listen and say, That must be hard. We learned to say sorry and to make up after a fight. We learned to persuade with kind reasons, and to ask questions that help people tell their stories. Most of all, we learned that words can heal hearts. A kind word can chase away a rain cloud. A sorry can clear the fog. A thank you can bring out the sun. So please, keep asking each other, How are you really feeling? Thank you, Heatherbrook!" Why did the rainbow have no colors? Because people had stopped sharing their feelings. What can a sorry do? It can clear the fog. Everyone claps. And then the purple stripe returned to the Great Rainbow! Red, orange, yellow, green, blue and purple. The rainbow is complete with six colors, and it shines over the whole highlands. Puff glows every color at once. At the very top of the hill, above the clouds, we see a huge hall. Inside, there is a big round table, and children from every continent are gathering around it. Who are they? Now, phonics. Let's review. Annoyed has double n. Turn to means go to someone for help. Congratulations goes high, then down. On cloud nine means very happy. Helpful ends in -ful. Thank you, Puff! Thank you, Grandma Rowan! Goodbye, Highlands of the Heart! | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, That must be hard for you. I should have noticed sooner. Today is the Heart Festival on Heart Hill! | 1140 | `conversation.aiTask.starterEn` | Welcome to the Heart Festival! The whole village is here, and the Great Rainbow is waiting. What did you learn in the Highlands of the Heart? | Let's pretend I'm Puff. Welcome to the Heart Festival! The whole village is here, and the Great Rainbow is waiting. What did you learn in the Highlands of the Heart? | A+B (block rule) | the app shows the partner child (its own face and name) saying the starter, so a starter speaking as Nib / Saffron / the Well / Pell / Granny Willa / the wolf / Puff / Grandma Rowan / Mr. Bram reads as the wrong speaker. The goal casts the AI in that role, so keep it and make the pretend play explicit (W25-36 rule). I'm Nib / I'm Pell / I'm Puff / your host, Puff folded into the frame; a second Let's -> it's time to / We can / Imagine; L1103 25-word sentence split |
